The Weyl Dimension Formula
[
Wey39
]
counts the number of semi-standard Young tableaux of a certain shape
\lambda
and thus gives the dimension of the corresponding irreducible representation.

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
\lambda
be a Young diagram. The number of semi-standard Young tableaux of shape
\lambda
is

\displaystyle\dim\left(V_{U(N)}^{\lambda}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\prod_{j=i+1}^{N}\frac{(\lambda_{i}-i)-(\lambda_{j}-j)}{j-i}

The Partition Algebra

The partition algebra
P_{t}(N)
is semisimple whenever
N>2t-2

[
HJ20
]
. In this regime, the irreps of
P_{t}(N)
are indexed by
N
-box Young diagrams such that all but at most
t
boxes are in the first row [
[
HR05
]
, Theorem 2.24b], i.e.

\widehat{P_{t}(N)}=\{\lambda:|\lambda|=N,\;\lambda_{0}\geq N-t\}

(3.19)

We will adopt the convention that when
\lambda
refers to an irrep of the partition algebra, the first row is ignored. With this convention, the irreps of the partition algebra are instead indexed by all Young diagrams with at most
t
boxes, i.e.

\widehat{P_{t}(N)}=\{\lambda:|\lambda|\leq t\}

(3.20)

With this convention, the dimension of an irrep
\lambda\in\widehat{P_{t}(N)}
is
[
HJ20
]
given as follows:

Lemma 3.28
.

\dim\left(V^{\lambda}_{P_{t}(N)}\right)=f^{\lambda}\sum_{i=|\lambda|}^{t}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{t}{i}\binom{i}{|\lambda|}

(3.21)

where
f^{\lambda}
is the hook length formula in
Lemma

3.24
, and
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{t}{i}
is a Stirling number of the second kind. Note that if
|\lambda|=t
, then
\dim\left(V^{\lambda}_{P_{t}(N)}\right)=f^{\lambda}
.

We will sometimes say an irrep
\lambda
is a
full box
irrep of
P_{t}(N)
if
|\lambda|=t
.

3.10
Clebsch-Gordan Coefficients in the Gelfand-Tsetlin Basis of Unitaries

Lemma 3.29

(
[
VK92
]
)
.

Let
G
be the unitary group
U(N)
. Let
X
be a semi-standard Young tableau of shape
\lambda
which may have positive and/or negative rows, and let
\boxed{x}
be a Young tableau of shape
\mu=\boxed{\phantom{x}}
, the standard irrep of
U(N)
.
We can write the Young tableau as a Gelfand-Tsetlin pattern, that is, a sequence of
N
shapes
(X^{\leq\ell})_{\ell\in[N]}
, where each
X^{\leq\ell}
is the shape of the
X
tableau when it is restricted to entries at most
\ell
(and removing
N-\ell
empty rows such that it has
\ell
rows). For each shape
X^{\leq\ell}
, let
X_{k}^{\leq\ell}
be the number of boxes on the
k
th
row (which may be positive or negative).

In the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis, the Clebsch-Gordan coefficient
\bra*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^+,r,X^+}C\ket*{\lambda,\mu,X,x}
for tensoring a single box Young tableau
\boxed{x}
has the following properties:

1.

The transition from shape
\lambda
to
\lambda^{+}
has no multiplicity, so
r
is redundant.

2.

It has non-zero value only when
\lambda^{+}
differs from
\lambda
by adding a single box (or removing a negative box)

3.

It has non-zero value only when
X^{+}
has the form

\displaystyle X^{+}=(X^{\leq 1},X^{\leq 2},\dots,X^{\leq x-1},X^{\leq x},X^{\leq x+1},\dots,X^{\leq N})+(0,0,\dots,0,\Box_{\Delta_{x}},\Box_{\Delta_{x+1}},\dots,\Box_{\Delta_{N}})\,,

where
“
+\Box_{\Delta_{z}}
”
means “add a box to the
\Delta_{z}
th
row” (or remove a negative box), for some
(\Delta_{x}\in[x],\dots,\Delta_{N}\in[N])
.
That is, every shape starting with the
x
th
one will have a net increase of one box.

4.

In this case, the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients can be written as a product of reduced Wigner coefficients, or scalar factors. Namely,

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\bra*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^+,r,X^+}C\ket*{\lambda,\mu,X,x}=\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{x-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x-1}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{=}\cdot\;\prod_{z>x}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k-1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}(-1)^{\Delta_{z}>\Delta_{z-1}}

Similarly, in the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis, the dual Clebsch-Gordan coefficients

\bra*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^-,r,X^-}dC\ket*{\lambda,\mu,X,x}=\bra*{\lambda,\bar{\mu},\lambda^-,r,X^-}C\ket*{\lambda,\bar{\mu},X,x}

(where
\bar{\mu}
is the dual of the standard representation) have the properties:

1.

The transition from shape
\lambda
to
\lambda^{-}
has no multiplicity.

2.

It has non-zero value only when
\lambda^{-}
differs from
\lambda
by removing a single box (or adding a negative box)

3.

It has non-zero value only when
X^{-}
has the form

\displaystyle X^{-}=(X^{\leq 1},X^{\leq 2},\dots,X^{\leq x-1},X^{\leq x},X^{\leq x+1},\dots,X^{\leq N})-(0,0,\dots,0,\Box_{\Delta_{x}},\Box_{\Delta_{x+1}},\dots,\Box_{\Delta_{N}})\,,

where
“
-\Box_{\Delta_{z}}
”
means “remove a box from the
\Delta_{z}
th
row” (or add a negative box), for some
(\Delta_{x}\in[x],\dots,\Delta_{N}\in[N])
.
That is, every shape starting with the
x
th
one will have a net decrease of one box.

4.

In this case, as before, the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients can be written as a product of reduced Wigner coefficients, or scalar factors. Namely,

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\bra*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^-,r,X^-}dC\ket*{\lambda,\mu,X,x}=\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{x-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x-1}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{=}\cdot\;\prod_{z>x}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k+1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k+1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}(-1)^{\Delta_{z}>\Delta_{z-1}}

3.11
The Fourier Basis of Semisimple Algebras

Definition 3.30

(The Fourier Basis)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a finite-dimensional semisimple algebra, i.e.

\mathcal{A}\overset{f}{\simeq}\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{End}(V^{\lambda})

(3.22)

A
Fourier basis

\left\{E_{i,j}^{\lambda}\right\}_{\lambda,i,j}
for
\mathcal{A}
is the preimage of the natural matrix unit basis
\{\outerproduct{i}{j}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda,i,j}
under the isomorphism
f
.

In general, the isomorphism in
Eq.

3.22
is not unique, and so the Fourier basis is only defined relative to
f
. Moreover, the matrix unit basis
\{\outerproduct{i}{j}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda,i,j}
implicitly assumes a choice of basis for each irrep space
V^{\lambda}
.
38
38

38

In algorithmic representation theory, a standard choice of basis for each irrep space is a
subalgebra adapted basis

[
DR90
,
MRR03
,
BCH05
,
MRW16
,
FND26
]
, which is constructed according to the branching rules when restricting an irrep of
\mathcal{A}
to an irrep of a subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
. We will not require this assumption.

Lemma 3.31

(
[
FND26
]
)
.

Let

\mathcal{A}_{k}

be a finite-dimensional semisimple algebra, and let

\left\{E_{i,j}^{k,\lambda}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{l}\lambda\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\\
i,j\in\mathcal{B}(V_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}^{\lambda})\end{subarray}}

be a Fourier basis.
Then, we have that

1.

[
FND26
, Lemma 2.13b]
:

\left(E_{i,j}^{k,\lambda}\right)^{*}=\frac{1}{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}^{\lambda})}E_{j,i}^{k,\lambda}

, where
*
denotes the dual basis element (
Definition

3.17
).

2.

[
FND26
, Section 4.5.2]
:

S\left(E_{i,j}^{k,\lambda}\right)^{T}=S\left(E_{j,i}^{k,\lambda}\right)

, where
S(\cdot)
denotes the Schur representation (
Definition

3.16
).

3.

[
FND26
, Lemma 2.13a]
:

E_{i,j}^{k,\lambda}E_{\ell,m}^{k,\mu}=\delta_{\lambda\mu}\,\delta_{j\ell}\,E_{i,m}^{k,\lambda}
.

3.12
Ratios of Irrep Dimensions for the Partition and Symmetric Group Algebras

Lemma 3.32
.

Consider the
(S_{N},P_{t}(N))
-Schur duality, where
t^{2}\leq N/4
(in this range,
P_{t}(N)
is always semisimple).
For any Young diagram
\lambda
with a full number of boxes
|\lambda|=t
,

\displaystyle\frac{\dim\left(V^{\lambda}_{P_{t}(N)}\right)}{\dim\left(V^{(N-t,\lambda)}_{S_{N}}\right)}=\frac{t!}{N^{t}}\left(1\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\right)\,,

(3.23)

where
(N-t,\lambda)
is the Young diagram with an extra first row of
N-t
boxes.

Similarly, for the
(U(N),\mathbb{C}[S_{t}])
-Schur duality, we have that for all irreps,
39
39

39

Note that in this case,
all
irreps correspond to Young diagrams with a full number of boxes (
|\lambda|=t\;\;\forall\lambda
).

\displaystyle\frac{\dim\left(V^{\lambda}_{\mathbb{C}[S_{t}]}\right)}{\dim\left(V^{\lambda}_{U(N)}\right)}=\frac{t!}{N^{t}}\left(1\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\right)

(3.24)

Proof.

When
|\lambda|=t
, we have that

\displaystyle\dim\left(V^{(N-t,\lambda)}_{S_{N}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{N!}{\prod_{(i,j)\in\lambda}h_{\lambda}(i,j)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N-t}\left(N-t-i+1+\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\right)}\,,

(
Lemma

3.25
)

\displaystyle\dim\left(V^{\lambda}_{P_{t}(N)}\right)

\displaystyle=\dim\left(V^{\lambda}_{S_{t}}\right)=f^{\lambda}=\frac{t!}{\prod_{(i,j)\in\lambda}h_{\lambda}(i,j)}\,,

(
Lemma

3.28
,
Lemma

3.25
)

\displaystyle\dim\left(V^{\lambda}_{U(N)}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{(i,j)\in\lambda}\frac{N+j-i}{h_{\lambda}(i,j)}\,.

(
Lemma

3.27
)

where
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}
is the
i
’th column length of
\lambda
.
Therefore, the irrep dimension ratio for the full box irreps of the
(S_{N},P_{t}(N))
duality is

\displaystyle\frac{\dim\left(V^{\lambda}_{P_{t}(N)}\right)}{\dim\left(V^{(N-t,\lambda)}_{S_{N}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{t!\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N-t}\left(N-t-i+1+\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\right)}{N!}

(3.25)

\displaystyle=\frac{t!\cdot(N-t)!\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N-t}\left(N-t-i+1+\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\right)}{N!\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N-t}\left(N-t-i+1\right)}

(3.26)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\binom{N}{t}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N-t}\left(\frac{N-t-i+1+\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}{N-t-i+1}\right)

(3.27)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\binom{N}{t}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\frac{N-t-i+1+\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}{N-t-i+1}\right)

(
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}=0
for
i>t
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\binom{N}{t}}\cdot\left(1\pm O\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)\right)^{t}

(
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\leq|\lambda|
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\binom{N}{t}}\cdot\left(1\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\right)

(
t^{2}=O(N)
)

\displaystyle=\frac{t!}{N^{t}}\cdot\left(1\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\right)

(3.28)

For the
(U(N),\mathbb{C}[S_{t}])
duality,

\displaystyle\frac{\dim\left(V^{\lambda}_{S_{t}}\right)}{\dim\left(V^{\lambda}_{U(N)}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{t!}{\prod_{(i,j)\in\lambda}(N+j-i)}

(3.29)

\displaystyle=\frac{t!}{N^{t}}\cdot\left(1\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\right)

(
|j-i|<|\lambda|
)

∎

4
The Tableau Recording Oracle

Let
G
be a compact Lie group and let
\rho:G\rightarrow U(N)
be a finite-dimensional unitary representation of
G
. We wish to give an efficient simulation of query access to
\rho(g)
for
g
sampled from the Haar measure of
G
(where
g
is re-used for all queries). In fact, we wish to simulate (controlled) access to all of
\rho(g),\rho(g)^{\dagger},\rho(g)^{*}
, and
\rho(g)^{\top}
.

4.1
The Uncompressed Representation Oracle

We first consider the following purification of the above process:

•

The adversary’s register is
V=\mathbb{C}^{N}
, containing the input on which they wish to evaluate
\rho(g)
.

•

The recording register is the
Hilbert space
L^{2}(G)

(which may be countably infinite-dimensional),
initialized to the Haar measure
dg=\ket{\bf 1}\in L^{2}(G)
using the constant
f(g)=1
function.

•

A query is represented by the unitary

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}\Big(\ket{x}\otimes\ket{f}\Big)=\int_{G}f(g)\cdot\rho(g)\ket{x}\otimes\ket{g}dg.

(4.1)

Since each
\rho(g)
is a unitary, this is in fact a norm-preserving transformation. We also use the following notation to denote
Q_{\rho}
:

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}=\int_{G}\rho(g)\otimes\outerproduct{g}{g}dg.

(4.2)

In fact, using
Q_{\rho}
, we can also easily describe a process that has query access to
\rho(g),\rho(g)^{\dagger},\rho(g)^{*}
, and
\rho(g)^{\top}
:
40
40

40

See
Section

3.3.1
for the definitions of
\mathsf{Inv}
,
\mathsf{Conj}
, and
\mathsf{Transpose}
.

•

Controlled access to
\rho(g)^{\dagger}
is simulated by (controlled)
\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Inv}\Big)Q_{\rho}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Inv}\Big)
,

•

Controlled access to
\rho(g)^{*}
is simulated by (controlled)
\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Conj}\Big)Q_{\rho}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Conj}\Big)
,

•

Controlled access to
\rho(g)^{\top}
is simulated by (controlled)
\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Transpose}\Big)Q_{\rho}\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Transpose}\Big)
.

In this section, our goal is to give an equivalent simulation of this oracle using the Clebsch-Gordan transform. To do this, we must first decompose
\rho
into irreducible representations, i.e.,
V\simeq\bigoplus_{\mu}M^{\mu}\otimes V_{G}^{\mu}
, where each
\mu\in\widehat{G}
is an irreducible representation that appears in
V
with multiplicity
\dim(M^{\mu})
. We represent vectors
\ket{\psi}\in V
using the basis
\ket{\mu}\otimes\ket{m}\otimes\ket{x}
for
\ket{m}\in M^{\mu}
and
\ket{x}\in V_{G}^{\mu}
.

In this basis, we can re-write
Q_{\rho}
as

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}=\sum_{\mu}\Big(\outerproduct*{\mu}{\mu}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{M_{\mu}}\otimes\int_{G}\rho_{\mu}(g)\otimes\outerproduct*{g}{g}dg\Big)=\sum_{\mu}\outerproduct*{\mu}{\mu}\otimes\mathbf{I}\otimes Q_{\rho_{\mu}}.

(4.3)

4.2
The Tableau Recording Oracle

The uncompressed recording oracle uses an extremely large, potentially even infinite-dimensional, Hilbert space in its purification, and is thus certainly not efficient in general. However, it turns out that in the Fourier basis (that is, considering the image of
L^{2}(G)
under the Fourier transform
F
) we get a recording oracle that is efficient whenever the Clebsch–Gordan transform
C
is efficient!
Indeed, when zero queries have been made, the recording register is in the trivial irrep state
F\cdot\ket{\bf 1}=\ket{\lambda= \mathsf{triv}, X=1, Y=1}
, because
\ket{\bf 1}\in L^{2}(G)
is the unique normalized state that is invariant under
G
-translation.

Formally, we define the tableau recording oracle
Q_{\rho}^{\mathrm{Tab}}

[
GY25
]
as follows.

•

As before, the adversary’s register contains linear combinations of
\ket{\mu}\otimes\ket{m}\otimes\ket{x}
for
\ket{m}\in M^{\mu}
and
\ket{x}\in V_{G}^{\mu}
.

•

The recording register now contains the following sub-registers:

–

A register containing a label
\lambda\in\widehat{G}
of an irreducible representation
\rho_{\lambda}
. While there are infinitely many such
\lambda
, it turns out that after
t
queries to the tableau recording oracle, at most
N^{t}
such
\lambda
are “reachable,” resulting in a finite-dimensional Hilbert space after any finite number of queries, which can be encoded in at most
t\log(N)
qubits.

–

Two registers
X
and
Y
containing elements of
V_{\lambda}
and
V_{\lambda}^{*}
controlled on
\ket{\lambda}
. We also non-canonically identify
V_{\lambda}^{*}
with
V_{\lambda}
by choosing a fixed basis of
V_{\lambda}
with corresponding dual basis of
V_{\lambda}^{*}
.

V_{\lambda}
has dimension at most
N^{t}
for any
\lambda
reachable after
t
queries, and can also therefore be encoded in at most
t\log(N)
qubits.

•

The update rule is given by the composition of the following two maps (see
Figure

12
):

–

First, given
\ket{\mu, m, x}\otimes\ket{\lambda, X, Y}
, apply
C\ket{\lambda, \mu, X, x}
. This results in a superposition of states of the form
\ket{\lambda, \mu, \lambda^+, r, X^+}
(see
Definition

3.19
).

–

Then, on the registers containing
\lambda^{+},\mu,\lambda,r,Y
, apply
dC^{\dagger}
,
the inverse of the dual Clebsch–Gordan transform.
This results in a superposition of states of the form
\ket{\lambda^+, \mu, Y^+, y}
.

–

The registers containing
\ket{\mu, m, y}
are returned to the adversary, while the registers containing
\ket{\lambda^+, X^+, Y^+}
are the updated state of the recording oracle.

4.2.1
Equivalence of the uncompressed and tableau recording oracles

Having defined our two recording oracles, we are ready to state the main result of this section.

Theorem 4.1

(Clebsch–Gordan Updates for Tableau Recordings)
.

Let
F:L^{2}(G)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*}
denote the Fourier transform. Then, the uncompressed representation oracle is isometric to the tableau recording oracle via the map
F
: in particular,
(\mathbf{I}\otimes F)\cdot Q_{\rho}\cdot(\mathbf{I}\otimes F)^{-1}
is equal to the tableau recording oracle update rule of a Clebsch–Gordan transform followed by an inverse dual Clebsch–Gordan transform.

This theorem is similar to the main result of
[
GY25
]
, but additionally proves that the Fourier transform is an explicit Uhlmann transformation relating the tableau oracle to the uncompressed oracle.

Proof.

To show this, we expand out

\displaystyle\bra{\mu,m,y,\lambda^+,X^+,Y^+}(\mathbf{I}\otimes F)\cdot Q_{\rho}\cdot(\mathbf{I}\otimes F)^{\dagger}\ket{\mu,m,x, \lambda, X,Y}

(4.4)

using the following expressions:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{I}\otimes F)^{\dagger}\ket{\mu,x, \lambda, X,Y}

\displaystyle=\ket{\mu,x}\otimes\Bigg(\sqrt{\dim(V_{G}^{\lambda})}\int\bra{Y}\rho_{\lambda}(g)\ket{X}\cdot\ket{g}\Bigg)

(4.5)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{I}\otimes F)^{\dagger}\ket{\mu,y,\lambda^+,X^+,Y^+}

\displaystyle=\ket{\mu,y}\otimes\Bigg(\sqrt{\dim(V_{G}^{\lambda^{+}})}\int\bra{Y^+}\rho_{\lambda^{+}}(g)\ket{X^+}\cdot\ket{g}\Bigg).

(4.6)

Plugging in the definition of
Q
, these imply that

\displaystyle(\ref{eq:goal})=

\displaystyle\int_{G}\bra{\mu,m,y}\Big(\outerproduct{\mu, m}{\mu, m}\otimes\rho_{\mu}(g)\Big)\ket{\mu,m,x}

(4.7)

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigg(\sqrt{\text{d}(V_{G}^{\lambda})\cdot\text{d}(V_{G}^{\lambda^{+}})}\bra{Y^+}\overline{\rho_{\lambda^{+}}(g)}\ket{X^+}\bra{Y}\rho_{\lambda}(g)\ket{X}\Bigg)dg

(4.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\text{d}(V_{G}^{\lambda})\cdot\text{d}(V_{G}^{\lambda^{+}})}\int_{G}\bra{Y^+}\overline{\rho_{\lambda^{+}}(g)}\ket{X^+}\underbrace{\bra{y}\rho_{\mu}(g)\ket{x}\bra{Y}\rho_{\lambda}(g)\ket{X}}_{(*)}dg

(4.9)

Let us expand out

\displaystyle(*)

\displaystyle=\bra{\lambda,Y,\mu,y}\Big(\outerproduct{\lambda}{\lambda}\otimes\rho_{\lambda}(g)\otimes\outerproduct{\mu}{\mu}\otimes\rho_{\mu}(g)\Big)\ket{\lambda,X,\mu,x}

(4.10)

\displaystyle=\bra{\lambda,\mu,Y,y}C^{\dagger}\cdot\Bigg(C\Big(\outerproduct{\lambda}{\lambda}\otimes\outerproduct{\mu}{\mu}\otimes\rho_{\lambda}(g)\otimes\rho_{\mu}(g)\Big)C^{\dagger}\Bigg)\cdot C\ket{\lambda,\mu,X,x}

(4.11)

\displaystyle=\bra*{\lambda,\mu,Y,y}C^{\dagger}\cdot\Bigg(\outerproduct{\lambda}{\lambda}\otimes\outerproduct{\mu}{\mu}\otimes\sum_{\lambda^{\#},r\in[m_{\lambda,\mu}^{\lambda^{\#}}]}\outerproduct{r}{r}\otimes\outerproduct*{\lambda^{\#}}{\lambda^{\#}}\otimes\rho_{\lambda^{\#}}(g)\Bigg)\cdot C\ket*{\lambda, \mu, X, x}

(4.12)

\displaystyle=\bra*{\lambda,\mu,Y,y}C^{\dagger}\cdot\Bigg(\outerproduct{\lambda}{\lambda}\otimes\outerproduct{\mu}{\mu}\otimes\sum_{\lambda^{\#},r\in[m_{\lambda,\mu}^{\lambda^{\#}}]}\outerproduct{r}{r}\otimes\outerproduct*{\lambda^{\#}}{\lambda^{\#}}

(4.13)

\displaystyle\hskip 93.89418pt\otimes\sum_{X^{\#},Y^{\#}}\outerproduct*{Y^{\#}}{Y^{\#}}\rho_{\lambda^{\#}}(g)\outerproduct*{X^{\#}}{X^{\#}}\Bigg)\cdot C\ket*{\lambda, \mu, X, x}

(4.14)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda^{\#},X^{\#},Y^{\#},\\
r\in[m_{\lambda,\mu}^{\lambda^{\#}}]\end{subarray}}\bra*{\lambda,\mu,Y,y}C^{\dagger}\ket*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^{\#},r,Y^{\#}}\cdot\bra*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^{\#},r,X^{\#}}C\ket*{\lambda,\mu,X,x}\cdot\bra*{Y^{\#}}\rho_{\lambda^{\#}}(g)\ket*{X^{\#}}

(4.15)

Plugging this back into (
4.9
), we have

\displaystyle(\ref{eq:goal})=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\text{d}(V_{G}^{\lambda})}{\text{d}(V_{G}^{\lambda^{+}})}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda^{\#},X^{\#},Y^{\#},\\
r\in[m_{\lambda,\mu}^{\lambda^{\#}}]\end{subarray}}\bra*{\lambda,\mu,Y,y}C^{\dagger}\ket*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^{\#},r,Y^{\#}}\bra*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^{\#},r,X^{\#}}C\ket*{\lambda,\mu,X,x}

(4.16)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\cdot\underbrace{\Bigg(\text{d}(V_{G}^{\lambda^{+}})\cdot\int_{G}\bra{Y^+}\overline{\rho_{\lambda^{+}}(g)}\ket{X^+}\bra*{Y^{\#}}\rho_{\lambda^{\#}}(g)\ket*{X^{\#}}dg\Bigg)}_{1\ \text{if}\ (\lambda^{+},X^{+},Y^{+})=(\lambda^{\#},X^{\#},Y^{\#}),\ \text{else}\ 0}

(4.17)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\text{d}(V_{G}^{\lambda})}{\text{d}(V_{G}^{\lambda^{+}})}}\sum_{r\in[m_{\lambda,\mu}^{\lambda^{+}}]}\bra*{\lambda,\mu,Y,y}C^{\dagger}\ket*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^+,r,Y^+}\bra*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^+,r,X^+}C\ket*{\lambda,\mu,X,x}.

(4.18)

Now, we invoke the dagger of
Lemma

3.21
to obtain

\displaystyle(\ref{eq:goal})=

\displaystyle\sum_{r\in[m_{\lambda^{+},\lambda,\mu}]}\bra*{\lambda^+,\mu,Y^+,y}dC^{\dagger}\ket*{\lambda^+,\mu,\lambda,r,Y}\bra*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^+,r,X^+}C\ket*{\lambda,\mu,X,x}.

(4.19)

Finally, we observe that these matrix entries correspond to the composition of the Clebsch–Gordan and inverse dual Clebsch–Gordan unitaries applied to the appropriate sub-registers indicated below:

\displaystyle C=

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{+},\mu,r,X,X^{+},x}\bra*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^+,r,X^+}C\ket*{\lambda,\mu,X,x}\cdot\outerproduct*{\lambda, \mu, \lambda^+, r, X^+}{\lambda, \mu, X, x}

(4.20)

\displaystyle dC^{\dagger}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{+},\mu,r,Y,Y^{+},y}\bra*{\lambda^+,\mu,Y^+,y}dC^{\dagger}\ket{\lambda^+,\mu,\lambda,r,Y}\cdot\outerproduct{\lambda^+, \mu, Y^+, y}{\lambda^+, \mu, \lambda, r, Y}.

(4.21)

This completes the derivation of the Clebsch–Gordan update rule.
∎

4.2.2
Other query types

The derived update rule implies that we can implement compressed conjugate, transpose, and inverse queries with the (linear
41
41

41

as opposed to anti-linear (or conjugate linear)

) isometries defined below

•

\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Conj}\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F^{\dagger}\Big)\cdot Q_{\rho}^{\mathrm{Tab}}\cdot\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Conj}\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F^{\dagger}\Big)
,

•

\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Transpose}\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F^{\dagger}\Big)\cdot Q_{\rho}^{\mathrm{Tab}}\cdot\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Transpose}\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F^{\dagger}\Big)
,

•

\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Inv}\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F^{\dagger}\Big)\cdot Q_{\rho}^{\mathrm{Tab}}\cdot\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Inv}\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F^{\dagger}\Big)
.

Using the three identities from
Section

3.3.1
, we can see that

•

\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Conj}\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F^{\dagger}\Big)
performs the map

\sum_{\lambda,X,Y}\alpha_{\lambda,X,Y}\ket{\lambda, X, Y}\mapsto\sum_{\lambda,X,Y}\overline{\alpha}_{\lambda,X,Y}\ket{\lambda^*, X, Y}.

•

\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Transpose}\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F^{\dagger}\Big)
performs the map

\sum_{\lambda,X,Y}\alpha_{\lambda,X,Y}\ket{\lambda, X, Y}\mapsto\sum_{\lambda,X,Y}\overline{\alpha}_{\lambda,X,Y}\ket{\lambda, Y, X}.

•

\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{Inv}\Big)\Big(\mathbf{I}\otimes F^{\dagger}\Big)
performs the map

\sum_{\lambda,X,Y}\alpha_{\lambda,X,Y}\ket{\lambda, X, Y}\mapsto\sum_{\lambda,X,Y}\alpha_{\lambda,X,Y}\ket{\lambda^*, Y, X}.

Thus, we conclude that

•

Conjugate queries can be implemented using the
complex conjugate

\overline{Q_{\rho}^{\mathrm{Tab}}}
of
Q_{\rho}^{\mathrm{Tab}}
conjugated by the linear isometry
\ket{\lambda, X, Y}\mapsto\ket{\lambda^*, X, Y}
.

4.2.3
Efficient Implementation for the Unitary Group

We now show how to efficiently implement the update rule,
Q_{\rho}^{\mathrm{Tab}}
, in
Theorem

4.1
for the case of Haar random unitaries,
G=U(N)
.
The Clebsch–Gordan transform for unitaries was shown to have an efficient algorithm in
[
BCH05
,
Har05
,
BFG+25
]
, and this algorithm was extended to the mixed unitary case, in which
\rho(U)
could be either the standard irrep or the dual irrep, by
[
Ngu23
,
FTH23
,
GBO23
]
. For the case of the irrep
\lambda
consisting of a tableau containing at most
t
boxes (either positive or negative), and the oracle representation being a single-box irrep (either the standard or dual irrep), they show how to implement the Clebsch–Gordan transform with error at most
\varepsilon
in time
\poly(\log(N),t,\log(\frac{1}{\epsilon}))
.

We show here that the Clebsch–Gordan transform implemented by these algorithms, or any similar algorithm that works in the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis, implies a corresponding dual Clebsch–Gordan algorithm with the same complexity which satisfies
Lemma

3.21
and can be used in
Theorem

4.1
.

Definition 4.2

(Signed Flipping of a Young Tableau)
.

For every basis state
\ket{X}
given by a semi-standard Young tableau
X
(allowing negative boxes), let
\mathsf{Flip}\ket{X}
be the operation that, up to a
\pm 1
sign, flips the Young tableau
X
by rotating it 180 degrees, for example, sending

\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
X
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}

\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
{\overline{X}}
\mathsf{Flip}

(4.22)

with a phase of
(-1)^{Nt+p}
where
p
is the
total parity
(even or odd) of all the entries in the tableau.
(Equivalently, the phase is
(-1)^{\sum_{x_{i}\in X}(N-x_{i})}
where the sum runs over all the entries in the boxes of the tableau.)

For instance, in the example in
Equation

4.22
above, there are 6 odd entries (namely
1
,
5
,
3
,
5
,
3
, and
3
), so the sum is even, and
t=11
is odd, so in this case we would apply a
-1
phase whenever
N
is odd.

When working with a semi-standard Young tableau
X
, we always apply a sign when flipping. Therefore, when clear from context, we refer to the signed flipping of a tableau as simply the
“flipping operation”
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
G=U(N)
, and let
\mu=\boxed{\phantom{x}}
be the standard irrep of
U(N)
.
The dual Clebsch–Gordan transform implied by
Lemma

3.29
is equivalent to applying the signed flip of
Definition

4.2
to the Young tableau
X
(and a corresponding unsigned flip to corresponding shape
\lambda
), applying the standard Clebsch–Gordan transform of
Lemma

3.29
, and then (signed) flipping it back.

That is, we have that

\displaystyle\bra*{\lambda,\mu,\lambda^-,X^-}dC\ket*{\lambda,\mu,X,x}=\langle\lambda,\mu,\lambda^{-},X^{-}|\,\mathsf{Flip}\cdot C\cdot\mathsf{Flip}\,|\lambda,\mu,X,x\rangle\,.

(4.23)

Where the
\mathsf{Flip}
applies to the
X
register, and also flips
\lambda
and
\lambda^{-}
(without sign) accordingly.

This means that every efficient algorithm for the Clebsch–Gordan transform implies a corresponding efficient dual Clebsch–Gordan transform algorithm where we
\mathsf{Flip}
, apply Clebsch–Gordan, and then
\mathsf{Flip}
back.

Proof.

We can show that the reduced Wigner coefficients, or scalar factors, of
Lemma

3.29
match up term by term (up to a
\pm 1
phase, which we will handle separately).
Let
Z={\overline{X}}
be the
unsigned
42
42

42

We are starting with the unsigned flip, which we then use to compute the necessary sign. This is the only time we take the flip to be unsigned.

flip of
X
. Then
Z_{k}^{\leq z}=-X_{z-k+1}^{\leq z}
(a positive row turns into a negative row and vice versa, and the row counting is reversed to go from
z
down to
1
rather than
1
to
z
).
We can see in
Lemma

3.29
that the Clebsch–Gordan scalar factors for
Z
are zero in the same places as for the dual Clebsch–Gordan, so it suffices to consider nonzero scalar factors.

First scalar factor

The first scalar factor of the Clebsch–Gordan coefficient of adding

(0,\allowbreak 0,\allowbreak\dots,\allowbreak 0,\allowbreak\Box_{\Delta_{x}},\allowbreak\Box_{\Delta_{x+1}},\allowbreak\dots,\allowbreak\Box_{\Delta_{N}})

to
Z

is

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{x-1}\left(Z_{k}^{\leq x-1}-Z_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\left(Z_{k}^{\leq x}-Z_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{x-1}\left(-X_{x-k}^{\leq x-1}+X_{x-\Delta_{x}+1}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\left(-X_{x-k+1}^{\leq x}+X_{x-\Delta_{x}+1}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{x-1}\left(-X_{k}^{\leq x-1}+X_{x-\Delta_{x}+1}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-x+k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
x-k+1\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\left(-X_{k}^{\leq x}+X_{x-\Delta_{x}+1}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-x+k-1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(by re-indexing

k\to x-k
in numerator and
k\to x-k+1
in denominator
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{x-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x-1}-X_{x-\Delta_{x}+1}^{\leq x}+(x-\Delta_{x}+1)-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq(x-\Delta_{x}+1)\end{subarray}}^{x}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x}-X_{x-\Delta_{x}+1}^{\leq x}+(x-\Delta_{x}+1)-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

(4.26)

When we now relabel
\Delta_{x}\to(x-\Delta_{x}+1)
(since each row of the Gelfand-Tsetlin pattern is reversed when we apply the flipping operation), we can see that this becomes identical to the first scalar factor of the dual Clebsch–Gordan coefficient of subtracting

(0,\allowbreak 0,\allowbreak\dots,\allowbreak 0,\allowbreak\Box_{\big(x-\Delta_{x}+1\big)},\allowbreak\Box_{\big((x+1)-\Delta_{(x+1)}+1\big)},\allowbreak\dots,\allowbreak\Box_{\big(N-\Delta_{N}+1\big)})

from
X
.

Remaining scalar factors

A similar argument holds for the other scalar factors. For the Clebsch–Gordan coefficient of adding

(0,\allowbreak 0,\allowbreak\dots,\allowbreak 0,\allowbreak\Box_{\Delta_{x}},\allowbreak\Box_{\Delta_{x+1}},\allowbreak\dots,\allowbreak\Box_{\Delta_{N}})

to the tableau
Z={\overline{X}}
, they are

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{(z-1)}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(Z_{k}^{\leq z-1}-Z_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(Z_{k}^{\leq z}-Z_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(Z_{k}^{\leq z}-Z_{\Delta_{(z-1)}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{(z-1)}-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{(z-1)}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(Z_{k}^{\leq z-1}-Z_{\Delta_{(z-1)}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{(z-1)}-k-1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}{\scriptstyle(-1)^{\Delta_{z}>\Delta_{(z-1)}}}

(4.27)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt=\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{(z-1)}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(-X_{z-k}^{\leq z-1}+X_{z-\Delta_{z}+1}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(-X_{z-k+1}^{\leq z}+X_{z-\Delta_{z}+1}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(-X_{z-k+1}^{\leq z}+X_{z-\Delta_{(z-1)}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{(z-1)}-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{(z-1)}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(-X_{z-k}^{\leq z-1}+X_{z-\Delta_{(z-1)}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{(z-1)}-k-1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}{\scriptstyle(-1)^{\Delta_{z}>\Delta_{(z-1)}}}

(4.28)

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt=\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
z-k\neq\Delta_{(z-1)}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\!\!\!\!\!\!\left(-X_{k}^{\leq z-1}+X_{z-\Delta_{z}+1}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-z+k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
z-k+1\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\!\!\!\!\!\!\left(-X_{k}^{\leq z}+X_{z-\Delta_{z}+1}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-z+k-1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\hskip-2.84544pt\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
z-k+1\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\!\!\!\!\!\!\left(-X_{k}^{\leq z}+X_{z-\Delta_{(z-1)}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{(z-1)}-z+k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
z-k\neq\Delta_{(z-1)}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\!\!\!\!\!\!\left(-X_{k}^{\leq z-1}+X_{z-\Delta_{(z-1)}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{(z-1)}-z+k-1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.5359pt(-1)^{\Delta_{z}>\Delta_{(z-1)}}}

(4.29)

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt=\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq z-\Delta_{(z-1)}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\!\!\!\!\!\!\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{z-\Delta_{z}+1}^{\leq z}+(z-\Delta_{z}+1)-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq z-\Delta_{z}+1\end{subarray}}^{z}\!\!\!\!\!\!\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{z-\Delta_{z}+1}^{\leq z}+(z-\Delta_{z}+1)-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\hskip-2.84544pt\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq z-\Delta_{z}+1\end{subarray}}^{z}\!\!\!\!\!\!\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{z-\Delta_{(z-1)}}^{\leq z-1}+(z-\Delta_{(z-1)})-k+1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
z-k\neq\Delta_{(z-1)}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\!\!\!\!\!\!\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{z-\Delta_{(z-1)}}^{\leq z-1}+(z-\Delta_{(z-1)})-k+1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.5359pt(-1)^{\Delta_{z}>\Delta_{(z-1)}}}\,.

(4.30)

As before, we now relabel
\Delta_{z}\to(z-\Delta_{z}+1)
and
\Delta_{z-1}\to(z-\Delta_{z-1})
, and, up to a
\pm 1
phase, it becomes identical to the corresponding scalar factor of the dual Clebsch–Gordan coefficient of subtracting

(0,\dots,\Box_{\big(x-\Delta_{x}+1\big)},\Box_{\big((x+1)-\Delta_{(x+1)}+1\big)},\dots,\Box_{\big(N-\Delta_{N}+1\big)})

from
X
.

It now remains to handle the sign. For each scalar factor besides the first (which does not come with a sign), the attached sign
(after relabeling
\Delta_{z}\to(z-\Delta_{z}+1)
and
\Delta_{z-1}\to(z-\Delta_{z-1})
) is

(-1)^{(z-\Delta_{z}+1)>(z-\Delta_{(z-1)})}=(-1)^{\Delta_{z}<(\Delta_{(z-1)}+1)}=(-1)^{\Delta_{z}\leq\Delta_{(z-1)}}

for the (unsigned) flipped Clebsch–Gordan, but

(-1)^{\Delta_{z}>\Delta_{(z-1)}}

for the dual Clebsch–Gordan coefficient.
Since the conditions

\Delta_{z}\leq\Delta_{(z-1)}

and

\Delta_{z}>\Delta_{(z-1)}

are mutually exclusive, they always disagree, meaning that the scalar factor for our unsigned flipped Clebsch–Gordan always has the wrong sign.

The product of the scalar factors that makes up the Clebsch–Gordan coefficient has
N-x
such scalar factors, which means that we are off by a phase of
(-1)^{N-x}=(-1)^{N+x}
.
This is exactly the sign that we get by applying a phase of

(-1)^{\sum_{x_{i}\in X}(N-x_{i})}
both times that we flip. The phase from each value
x_{i}
in the tableau that is not added/removed will cancel between the flip before and after the
Clebsch–Gordan transform. The only phase remaining will be from the entries that are added or removed in between.

The Clebsch–Gordan transform of inserting
x
either adds a positive box containing
x
, removes a negative box containing
x
, or replaces a negative box containing
x
with
y
and adds positive box containing
y
. In any of these cases, when performing the signed flip before and after the Clebsch–Gordan transform, the resulting phase is
(-1)^{N-x}
as desired, completing the proof.
∎

Claim 4.4
.

Let
C
be the Clebsch–Gordan transform implied by
Lemma

3.29
, and let
dC
be the corresponding dual Clebsch–Gordan transform, given by applying
C
on the flipped tableau as in
Lemma

4.3
. Then
C
and
dC
satisfy
Lemma

3.21
. Namely,

\displaystyle\bra*{
\lambda^+,
\lambda,
X
}dC\ket*{
\lambda^+,
X^+,
x
}=\sqrt{\frac{\dim\left(V_{U(N)}^{\lambda}\right)}{\dim\left(V_{U(N)}^{\lambda^{+}}\right)}}\;\bra*{
\lambda,
\lambda^+,
X^+
}C\ket*{
\lambda,
X,
x
}

(4.31)

This implies that an efficient algorithm for
C
alone is enough to give an efficient perfect update algorithm for the tableau recording oracle for random unitaries.

Proof.

We first observe that each Clebsch–Gordan coefficient and its dual Clebsch–Gordan coefficient are non-zero on the same support.
Where they are both non-zero, we proceed by computing the ratio

\displaystyle\frac{\bra*{
\lambda,
\lambda^+,
X^+
}C\ket*{
\lambda,
X,
x
}}{\bra*{
\lambda^+,
\lambda,
X
}dC\ket*{
\lambda^+,
X^+,
x
}}

(4.32)

by evaluating the ratios of each scalar factor individually.

First scalar factor

The ratio of the first scalar factors of the two Clebsch–Gordan coefficients is

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{x-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x-1}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\left((X^{+})_{k}^{\leq x}-(X^{+})_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{x-1}\left((X^{+})_{k}^{\leq x-1}-(X^{+})_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.33)

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{x-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x-1}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{x-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x-1}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k-1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.34)

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\left(X_{k}^{\leq x}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.35)

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\frac{X_{k}^{\leq x}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k-1}{X_{k}^{\leq x}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.36)

Remaining scalar factors

The ratios of the remaining scalar factors of the two Clebsch–Gordan coefficients are

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k-1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}(-1)^{\Delta_{z}>\Delta_{z-1}}

(4.37)

\displaystyle\cdot

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left((X^{+})_{k}^{\leq z}-(X^{+})_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left((X^{+})_{k}^{\leq z-1}-(X^{+})_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left((X^{+})_{k}^{\leq z-1}-(X^{+})_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k+1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left((X^{+})_{k}^{\leq z}-(X^{+})_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k+1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}(-1)^{\Delta_{z}>\Delta_{z-1}}

(4.38)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k-1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.39)

\displaystyle\cdot

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k-1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.40)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k-1\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\left(X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k-1\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.41)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\frac{X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k-1}{X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\frac{X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k}{X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k-1}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.42)

Putting them together

We now combine these to get the ratio of the Clebsch–Gordan coefficients as the product of the ratios of the scalar factors:

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\frac{\bra*{
\lambda,
\lambda^+,
X^+
}C\ket*{
\lambda,
X,
x
}}{\bra*{
\lambda^+,
\lambda,
X
}dC\ket*{
\lambda^+,
X^+,
x
}}

(4.43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{x}\end{subarray}}^{x}\frac{X_{k}^{\leq x}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k-1}{X_{k}^{\leq x}-X_{\Delta_{x}}^{\leq x}+\Delta_{x}-k}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.44)

\displaystyle\cdot

\displaystyle\prod_{z=x+1}^{N}\left\lvert\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z}\end{subarray}}^{z}\frac{X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k-1}{X_{k}^{\leq z}-X_{\Delta_{z}}^{\leq z}+\Delta_{z}-k}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{z-1}\end{subarray}}^{z-1}\frac{X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k}{X_{k}^{\leq z-1}-X_{\Delta_{z-1}}^{\leq z-1}+\Delta_{z-1}-k-1}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.45)

This is a telescoping product, since in each factor of the outside product, the second part cancels with the first part of the previous factor, ultimately giving

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{N}\end{subarray}}^{N}\frac{X_{k}^{\leq N}-X_{\Delta_{N}}^{\leq N}+\Delta_{N}-k-1}{X_{k}^{\leq N}-X_{\Delta_{N}}^{\leq N}+\Delta_{N}-k}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.46)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{N}\end{subarray}}^{N}\frac{\lambda_{k}-(\lambda_{\Delta_{N}}+1)+\Delta_{N}-k}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{\Delta_{N}}+\Delta_{N}-k}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.47)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{N}\end{subarray}}^{N}\frac{\lambda^{+}_{k}-\lambda^{+}_{\Delta_{N}}+\Delta_{N}-k}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{\Delta_{N}}+\Delta_{N}-k}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.48)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq\Delta_{N}\end{subarray}}^{N}\frac{(\lambda^{+}_{k}-k)-(\lambda^{+}_{\Delta_{N}}-{\Delta_{N}})}{(\lambda_{k}-k)-(\lambda_{\Delta_{N}}-{\Delta_{N}})}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.49)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\prod_{i=1}^{\Delta_{N}-1}\frac{(\lambda^{+}_{i}-i)-(\lambda^{+}_{\Delta_{N}}-{\Delta_{N}})}{(\lambda_{i}-i)-(\lambda_{\Delta_{N}}-{\Delta_{N}})}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lvert\prod_{j=\Delta_{N}+1}^{N}\frac{(\lambda^{+}_{\Delta_{N}}-{\Delta_{N}})-(\lambda^{+}_{j}-j)}{(\lambda_{\Delta_{N}}-{\Delta_{N}})-(\lambda_{j}-j)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\underbrace{\left\lvert\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
i\neq\Delta_{N}\\
j\neq\Delta_{N}\end{subarray}}\frac{(\lambda^{+}_{i}-i)-(\lambda^{+}_{j}-j)}{(\lambda_{i}-i)-(\lambda_{j}-j)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}}_{=1\,\text{
since $\lambda$ and $\lambda^{+}$ only differ on row $\Delta_{N}$
}}

(4.50)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\prod_{i=1}^{N}\;\prod_{j=i+1}^{N}\frac{(\lambda^{+}_{i}-i)-(\lambda^{+}_{j}-j)}{(\lambda_{i}-i)-(\lambda_{j}-j)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\dim\left(V_{U(N)}^{\lambda^{+}}\right)}{\dim\left(V_{U(N)}^{\lambda}\right)}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.\qed

(4.52)

5
The Path Recording Oracle

The tableau-recording oracle gives an exact simulation of queries to a Haar-random group representation by storing the purification in the Fourier basis of
L^{2}(G)
. Unfortunately, the tableau-recording oracle can be challenging to interpret
operationally: from the contents of the tableau register, it can be difficult to characterize in simple terms what information about the queried representation has been learned or fixed by the algorithm.

The goal of this section is to construct an equivalent recording in a path basis. Informally, after

t
queries, we would like the recording register to store a superposition of Feynman path information
\ket{x_1,\ldots,x_t,y_1,\hdots,y_t}
.
For a fixed group element
g
, this path corresponds to the matrix element

\prod_{i=1}^{t}\langle y_{i}|\rho(g)|x_{i}\rangle=\langle y_{1},\ldots,y_{t}|\rho(g)^{\otimes t}|x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}\rangle,

and indeed, a non-adaptive algorithm that queries the uncompressed oracle
Q_{\rho}
on inputs
\ket{x_1, \hdots, x_t}
would result in the state

\sum_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{t}}\ket{y_1, \hdots, y_t}\otimes\int_{G}\bra{\vec y}\rho^{\otimes t}\ket{\vec x}\cdot\ket{g}dg.

How does this compare with recording the path
\ket{\vec x, \vec y}
? To make this precise, we introduce the matrix element map

\Theta_{t}:V^{\otimes t}\otimes\overline{V}^{\otimes t}\longrightarrow L^{2}(G)

defined as

\Theta_{t}|\vec{x},\vec{y}\rangle:=\int_{G}\langle\vec{y}|\rho(g)^{\otimes t}|\vec{x}\rangle|g\rangle\,dg,

(5.1)

so that

Q_{\rho}^{\otimes t}\ket{\vec x}=\sum_{\vec{y}\in\mathcal{B}(V)}\ket{\vec y}\otimes\Theta_{t}(\ket{\vec x}\ket{\vec y}).

(5.2)

Using this, we can see that for any particular representation
\rho
, path states contain some redundancies. Let

\mathcal{A}_{t}=\mathrm{End}_{G}(V^{\otimes t})

be the commutant algebra of the tensor-power representation. As discussed in the technical overview, since every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{t}
commutes
with
\rho(g)^{\otimes t}
, the matrix-element map satisfies

\Theta_{t}(A\otimes\mathbf{I})=\Theta_{t}(\mathbf{I}\otimes A^{T}).

Therefore, it should be the case that
(A\otimes\mathbf{I})\ket{\psi}=(\mathbf{I}\otimes A^{T})\ket{\psi}
on valid path-recording states. This identification is implemented by the commutant EPR projector
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
.

Furthermore, path states may require different relative normalizations. These factors are encoded in the reweighting operator
\Lambda_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
defined later.

With these ingredients, the path-recording update has the following form: append a new
input-output pair
(x_{t},y_{t})
, project the recording register onto the commutant-EPR subspace
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
,
and apply an appropriate Schur-Weyl subspace reweighting. The remainder of this section defines these
operators precisely, proves that the resulting path-recording oracle is isometric to the tableau
recording oracle, gives an interpretation of
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
in terms of the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{t}
(when it is semisimple), and derives forms of the update that are useful for adaptive query algorithms.

5.1
Defining the Path Recording Oracle

Let

\rho:G\to U(V)

be a finite-dimensional unitary representation of a compact Lie group
G
. Fix an orthonormal basis
\mathcal{B}(V)=\{\ket{x}\}
of
V
. We use the same ket notation
\ket{y}
for the corresponding conjugate basis of
\overline{V}
, specifying registers when needed.

For each
t
, let

\mathsf{Schur}_{t}:V^{\otimes t}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\lambda}V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}

be the generalized Schur transform for
V^{\otimes t}
, so that

\rho^{\otimes t}(g)=\bigoplus_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}(g)\otimes\mathbf{I}_{V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}}.

Define the ordered path space

\mathsf{Path}_{t}\coloneqq V^{\otimes t}\otimes\overline{V}^{\otimes t}.

A path basis vector is written

\ket{\vec x,\vec y}\coloneqq\ket{x_1,\ldots,x_t}\otimes\ket{y_1,\ldots,y_t}.

Note that
\ket{y_1, \ldots, y_t}
implicitly corresponds to the dual basis element
\ket{y_1^*, \ldots, y_t^*}\in\overline{V}^{\otimes t}
. Next, we define the
append
map

\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}:\mathsf{Path}_{t}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Path}_{t+1}

by

\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}\ket{\vec x,\vec y}=\ket{x_1,\ldots,x_t,x}\otimes\ket{y_1,\ldots,y_t,y}.

(5.3)

Equivalently,

\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}=\mathbf{I}\otimes\ket{x,y}.

We relate the path space
V^{\otimes t}\otimes\overline{V}^{\otimes t}
to the (Fourier/tableau) compressed oracle via the double Schur transform

\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}}:V^{\otimes t}\otimes\overline{V}^{\otimes t}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\left(V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}\right)\otimes\overline{\left(V_{G}^{\lambda^{\prime}}\otimes V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda^{\prime}}\right)}.

We write double-Schur basis vectors as

\ket{\lambda,X,T}\otimes\ket{\lambda',Y,R},

where

X\in\mathcal{B}(V_{G}^{\lambda}),\qquad Y\in\mathcal{B}(V_{G}^{\lambda^{\prime}}),\qquad T\in\mathcal{B}(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}),\qquad R\in\mathcal{B}(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda^{\prime}}).

This is again shorthand for the vector
\ket{\lambda,X,T}\otimes\overline{\ket{\lambda',Y,R}}
.

Let
\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda,t}
be the projector in the Schur basis onto block
\lambda
, that is, onto
V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}
. We then define its path basis version as

\Pi_{\lambda,t}:=\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\dagger}\cdot\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda,t}\cdot\mathsf{Schur}_{t}.

(5.4)

In a slight abuse of notation, we use
\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda,t}^{\otimes 2}
to denote
\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda,t}\otimes\overline{\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda,t}^{\otimes 2}}
, which is the projector in the
double-Schur basis onto the
(\lambda,\lambda)
block

\left(V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}\right)\otimes\overline{\left(V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}\right)}.

Equivalently,

\displaystyle\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda,t}^{\otimes 2}\left(\ket{\lambda_1,X,T}\otimes\ket{\lambda_2,Y,R}\right)=\begin{cases}\ket{\lambda,X,T}\otimes\ket{\lambda,Y,R},&\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\lambda,\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The corresponding path basis projector is then

\Pi_{\lambda,t}^{\otimes 2}:=\left(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\top}\right)\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda,t}^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}}\right).

For each
\lambda
, define the normalized EPR state on the pair of

\mathcal{A}_{t}
-registers by

\ket{\mathsf{EPR}_{\calA_t}^{\lambda}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}}\sum_{T\in\mathcal{B}(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}\ket{T}\otimes\ket{T}.

Let
\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{A}_{t}}_{\mathrm{EPR},t}
be the double-Schur-basis
projector that acts as zero on blocks
\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}
, and on the

\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}
block projects the two
\mathcal{A}_{t}
-registers onto

\ket{\mathsf{EPR}_{\calA_t}^{\lambda}}
while acting as identity on the
two
G
-registers. Equivalently,

\displaystyle\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{A}_{t}}_{\mathrm{EPR},t}\left(\ket{\lambda,X,T}\otimes\ket{\lambda',Y,R}\right)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{T,R}}{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{B}(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}\ket{\lambda,X,S}\otimes\ket{\lambda,Y,S},&\lambda=\lambda^{\prime},\\[8.61108pt]
0,&\lambda\neq\lambda^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Via the double Schur transform, this induces the following projector on the path space.

\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}:=\left(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\top}\right)\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{A}_{t}}_{\mathrm{EPR},t}\left(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}}\right).

(5.5)

Finally, define the subspace-reweighting operator

W_{t}:=\sum_{\lambda}\sqrt{\frac{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}{\dim(V_{G}^{\lambda})}}\Pi_{\lambda,t}^{\otimes 2}.

(5.6)

This operator reweights each double-Schur subspace with matching irrep label
\lambda
on the two sides.

On the equal-irrep subspace

\Pi_{\mathrm{diag},t}:=\sum_{\lambda}\Pi_{\lambda,t}^{\otimes 2},

the inverse reweighting is

W_{t}^{+}:=\sum_{\lambda}\sqrt{\frac{\dim(V_{G}^{\lambda})}{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}}\Pi_{\lambda,t}^{\otimes 2}.

(5.7)

Thus

W_{t}^{+}W_{t}=W_{t}W_{t}^{+}=\Pi_{\mathrm{diag},t}.

Since

\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}\preceq\Pi_{\mathrm{diag},t},

this is a valid inverse restricted to the legal path subspace.

5.2
Path Recording Theorem Statement

With these definitions set up, we are ready to state and analyze our path recording oracle.

Definition 5.1

(Path Recording Oracle)
.

The path recording oracle is defined to be the following linear operator. The input space of the operator is
V\otimes\bigoplus_{t}\mathsf{Path}_{t}
.

On subspace
V\otimes\mathsf{Path}_{t}
, the operator is defined as

Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}=\sum_{x,y\in\mathcal{B}(V)}\outerproduct{y}{x}\otimes W_{t+1}\cdot\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}\cdot\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}\cdot W_{t}^{+}\cdot\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}},

(5.8)

which outputs an element of
V\otimes\mathsf{Path}_{t+1}
.

When restricted to the legal path-recording subspace
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
, the operator is given by

Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}=\sum_{x,y\in\mathcal{B}(V)}\outerproduct{y}{x}\otimes W_{t+1}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}W_{t}^{+}.

(5.9)

Remark 5.2

(Equivalent definition)
.

Another definition of the path recording oracle is the following:

Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}=\sum_{x,y\in\mathcal{B}(V)}\outerproduct{y}{x}\otimes\Lambda_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}\cdot\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}\cdot\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}\cdot\Lambda_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{+}\cdot\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}},

(5.10)

where

\Lambda_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}=\sum_{\lambda}\sqrt{\frac{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}{\dim(V_{G}^{\lambda})}}\cdot\Big(\Pi_{\lambda,t}\otimes\mathbf{I}\Big)

(5.11)

and
\Lambda_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{+}
is inverse to
\Lambda_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
on
\im(\mathsf{Schur}_{t})
. This is equivalent because
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
in particular projects onto
\Pi_{\mathrm{diag},t}
, on which
\Lambda_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
and
W_{t}
are equivalent (as are their pseudoinverses).

We will prove that
Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}
is equivalent to
Q_{\rho}
on an appropriate subspace of legal “reachable” states. Let

\widehat{G}_{t}:=\mathrm{Irr}(\rho^{\otimes t})=\left\{\lambda\in\widehat{G}:\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})>0\right\}

be the set of irreps appearing in the Schur–Weyl decomposition of

V^{\otimes t}
, and let

\mathsf{Tab}_{t}:=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\widehat{G}_{t}}V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes\overline{V_{G}^{\lambda}}

be the
t
-query reachable tableau space.

Theorem 5.3

(Correctness of the path-recording oracle)
.

There is a sequence of partial isometries
U_{t}:\bigoplus_{\lambda}V^{\lambda}_{G}\otimes\overline{V}^{\lambda}_{G}\rightarrow\mathsf{Path}_{t}
such that the following holds. For every
t
,

Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}\cdot(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t}\cdot F)=(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t+1}\cdot F)\cdot Q_{\rho},

(5.12)

when inputs are restricted to
V\otimes F^{-1}(\mathsf{Tab}_{t})
.

Therefore, for every
t
-query quantum algorithm
A
whose query register is
V
, for all input states
\ket{\psi}
, we have that

A^{Q_{\rho}}(\ket{\psi})\simeq A^{Q_{\rho}^{\mathrm{Path}}}(\ket{\psi})

via a fixed partial isometry
U_{t}\cdot F
on the recording register.

Setup

With this notation, the path-recording update is given by

Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}=\sum_{x,y\in\mathcal{B}(V)}\outerproduct{y}{x}\otimes W_{t+1}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}W_{t}^{+}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}.

We will prove

Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t}F)=(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t+1}F)Q_{\rho}

on the appropriately restricted input states.

5.3
Defining the partial isometry
U_{t}

To begin proving
Theorem

5.3
, we first define the partial isometries

U_{t}:\bigoplus_{\lambda}V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes\overline{V_{G}^{\lambda}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Path}_{t}.

For every irrep
\lambda
, we write the basis
states of
V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes\overline{V}_{G}^{\lambda}
as
\ket{\lambda,X,Y}
.

With this setup, we have that

\im\Big(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}}\Big)=\bigoplus_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\widehat{G}_{t}}\left(V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}\right)\otimes\overline{\left(V_{G}^{\lambda^{\prime}}\otimes V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda^{\prime}}\right)}.

Since
\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}}
is an isometry, we thus conclude that

\Big(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}}\Big)^{\dagger}\Big(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}}\Big)=\mathbf{I}_{\mathsf{Path}_{t}},\qquad\Big(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}}\Big)\Big(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}}\Big)^{\dagger}=\Pi_{\im(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}})}.

Next, we define the
\mathcal{A}_{t}
EPR isometry

E_{t}:\bigoplus_{\lambda}V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes\overline{V}_{G}^{\lambda}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\lambda}\left(V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}\right)\otimes\overline{\left(V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}\right)}\subset\bigoplus_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\left(V_{G}^{\lambda}\otimes V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}\right)\otimes\overline{\left(V_{G}^{\lambda^{\prime}}\otimes V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda^{\prime}}\right)}

by

E_{t}\ket{\lambda,X,Y}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{B}(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}\ket{\lambda,X,R}\otimes\ket{\lambda,Y,R},\qquad\lambda\in\widehat{G}_{t}

and
E_{t}\ket{\lambda,X,Y}=0
otherwise. Since this operation is simply tensoring with
a normalized EPR state on the two
\mathcal{A}_{t}
-registers,
E_{t}
is an isometry when restricted to
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}
. Moreover, by construction,

\im(E_{t})\subset\im(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}})

The adjoint of
E_{t}
is given on double-Schur basis states by

E_{t}^{\dagger}\left(\ket{\lambda,X,T}\otimes\ket{\lambda',Y,R}\right)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}}\ket{\lambda,X,Y},&\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}\in\widehat{G}_{t}\text{ and }T=R,\\[8.61108pt]
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

With these definitions, it follows directly that

E_{t}^{\dagger}E_{t}=\Pi_{\mathsf{Tab}_{t}},\qquad E_{t}E_{t}^{\dagger}=\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{A}_{t}}_{\mathrm{EPR},t}.

Finally, we are ready to define the operators

U_{t}:=\left(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\top}\right)E_{t}.

To understand the domain and image on which
U_{t}
is an isometry, we calculate

U_{t}^{\dagger}U_{t}=E_{t}^{\dagger}\Pi_{\im(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}})}E_{t}=E_{t}^{\dagger}E_{t}=\Pi_{\mathsf{Tab}_{t}},

while

\displaystyle U_{t}U_{t}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}})^{\dagger}E_{t}E_{t}^{\dagger}(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}})

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}})^{\dagger}\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{A}_{t}}_{\mathrm{EPR},t}(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}})

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}},

where the last equation holds by definition of
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
. Thus, we have proved the following fact.

Claim 5.4
.

U_{t}
is an isometry between
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}
and the legal path subspace
\im(\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}})\subset\mathsf{Path}_{t}
.

As a result, we see that
U_{t}\cdot F
is an isometry between
F^{-1}(\mathsf{Tab}_{t})
and
\im(\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}})
. We next give a characterization of the subspace
F^{-1}(\mathsf{Tab}_{t})
.

5.4
Path Matrix Elements

To properly understand
U_{t}
, we consider the matrix element map

\Theta_{t}:\mathsf{Path}_{t}\longrightarrow L^{2}(G)

defined as

\Theta_{t}\ket{\vec x,\vec y}:=\int_{G}\bra{\vec y}\rho^{\otimes t}(g)\ket{\vec x}\ket{g}\,dg.

(5.13)

We make the following claims about the map
\Theta_{t}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

\im(F\cdot\Theta_{t}\cdot\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}})=\im(F\cdot\Theta_{t})=\mathsf{Tab}_{t}
. Moreover, as operators defined on
\mathsf{Path}_{t}
,

\displaystyle U_{t}\cdot F\cdot\Theta_{t}

\displaystyle=W_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}.

(5.14)

Proof.

We first prove that
\mathsf{Tab}_{t}\subset\im(F\cdot\Theta_{t}\cdot\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}})
. To prove this, let
\ket{\lambda, X, Y}\in\mathsf{Tab}_{t}
be a tableau basis vector. Let
\ket{T}\in V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}
be an arbitrary basis state, which we also identify with a basis state in
\overline{V}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}
. Then, we observe that

\displaystyle\Theta_{t}\Big(\mathsf{Schur}^{\dagger}\ket{\lambda, X, T}\otimes\mathsf{Schur}^{\top}\ket{\lambda, Y, T}\Big)

\displaystyle=\int_{G}\bra{\lambda, Y, T}\mathsf{Schur}\cdot\rho^{\otimes t}(g)\cdot\mathsf{Schur}^{\dagger}\ket{\lambda, X, T}\cdot\ket{g}dg

\displaystyle=\int_{G}\bra{\lambda, Y, T}\rho_{\lambda}(g)\ket{\lambda, X, T}\cdot\ket{g}dg

(5.15)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\dim(V_{G}^{\lambda})}}\cdot F^{-1}\ket{\lambda, X, Y}.

(5.16)

Since this holds for every basis state
\ket{T}
, we can initialize the
V^{\lambda}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}\otimes\overline{V}^{\lambda}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
to be an EPR state (instead of a fixed basis vector) and also conclude that
F^{-1}\ket{\lambda, X, Y}\in\im(\Theta_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}})
.

Next, we prove that
\im(F\cdot\Theta_{t})\subset\mathsf{Tab}_{t}
as well as
Eq.

5.14
. It suffices to prove both of these claims on basis vectors
\ket{\vec x,\vec y}\in\mathsf{Path}_{t}
. Expand the Schur transform as

\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\ket{\vec x}=\sum_{\lambda,X,T}S_{\vec{x}}^{\lambda,X,T}\ket{\lambda,X,T},

where

X\in\mathcal{B}(V_{G}^{\lambda}),\qquad T\in\mathcal{B}(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}).

Since Schur-Weyl duality exactly tells us that the representation
\rho^{\otimes t}
decomposes as

\rho^{\otimes t}(g)=\bigoplus_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}(g)\otimes\mathbf{I}_{V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda}},

we have that

\displaystyle\bra{\vec y}\rho^{\otimes t}(g)\ket{\vec x}=\sum_{\lambda,X,Y,T}\overline{S_{\vec{y}}^{\lambda,Y,T}}S_{\vec{x}}^{\lambda,X,T}\bra{Y}\rho_{\lambda}(g)\ket{X}.

Therefore, by the definition of the Fourier transform,

\displaystyle F\Theta_{t}\ket{\vec x,\vec y}=\sum_{\lambda,X,Y,T}\frac{\overline{S_{\vec{y}}^{\lambda,Y,T}}S_{\vec{x}}^{\lambda,X,T}}{\sqrt{\dim(V_{G}^{\lambda})}}\ket{\lambda,X,Y}.

Since the coefficient of
\ket{\lambda, X, Y}
is nonzero only if both
\ket{\lambda, X, T}
and
\ket{\lambda, Y, T}
are in the image of the Schur transform for some
T
, this tells us that
F\Theta_{t}\ket{\vec x, \vec y}\in\mathsf{Tab}_{t}
.

Next, applying
U_{t}=(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\top})E_{t}
gives

\displaystyle U_{t}F\Theta_{t}\ket{\vec x,\vec y}=\sum_{\lambda,X,Y,T,R}\frac{\overline{S_{\vec{y}}^{\lambda,Y,T}}S_{\vec{x}}^{\lambda,X,T}}{\sqrt{\dim(V_{G}^{\lambda})\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}}\left(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\top}\right)\left(\ket{\lambda,X,R}\otimes\ket{\lambda,Y,R}\right).

(5.17)

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{Schur}_{t}}\right)\ket{\vec x,\vec y}=\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime},X,Y,T,R}S_{\vec{x}}^{\lambda,X,T}\overline{S_{\vec{y}}^{\lambda^{\prime},Y,R}}\ket{\lambda,X,T}\otimes\ket{\lambda',Y,R}.

Projecting onto the EPR subspace gives

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}\ket{\vec x,\vec y}=\left(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\top}\right)\sum_{\lambda,X,Y,T,R}\frac{S_{\vec{x}}^{\lambda,X,T}\overline{S_{\vec{y}}^{\lambda,Y,T}}}{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}\ket{\lambda,X,R}\otimes\ket{\lambda,Y,R}.

The operator
W_{t}
multiplies the
\lambda
-subspace by

\sqrt{\frac{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}{\dim(V_{G}^{\lambda})}}.

Hence

\displaystyle W_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}\ket{\vec x,\vec y}=\sum_{\lambda,X,Y,T,R}\frac{\overline{S_{\vec{y}}^{\lambda,Y,T}}S_{\vec{x}}^{\lambda,X,T}}{\sqrt{\dim(V_{G}^{\lambda})\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}^{\lambda})}}\left(\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathsf{Schur}_{t}^{\top}\right)\left(\ket{\lambda,X,R}\otimes\ket{\lambda,Y,R}\right).

(5.18)

We conclude that

U_{t}F\Theta_{t}\ket{\vec x,\vec y}=W_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}\ket{\vec x,\vec y},

as desired.
∎

5.5
Proof of
Theorem

5.3

To prove
Theorem

5.3
, we derive an expression that is simultaneously equal to both
(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t+1}F)Q_{\rho}
and
Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t}F)
.

Beginning with the former, we claim the following operator identity holds on inputs
\ket{x}\otimes\ket{\vec x}\ket{\vec y}
.

Claim 5.6
.

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}\cdot(\ket{x}\otimes\Theta_{t})=\sum_{y}\ket{y}\otimes\Theta_{t+1}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}.

(5.19)

To see this, we simply observe that the left-hand side is equal to

Q_{\rho}\left(\ket{x}\otimes\int_{G}\bra{\vec y}\rho^{\otimes t}(g)\ket{\vec x}\ket{g}\,dg\right)=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{B}(V)}\ket{y}\otimes\int_{G}\bra{y}\rho(g)\ket{x}\bra{\vec y}{\rho^{\otimes t}(g)}\ket{\vec x}\ket{g}\,dg.

and the second tensor factor exactly contains the
\ket{\vec x, x,\vec y, y}
matrix element function.

Applying
\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t+1}F
to the recording register and invoking
Eq.

5.14
, we see that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t+1}F)Q_{\rho}\left(\ket{x}\otimes\Theta_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}\ket{y}\otimes U_{t+1}F\Theta_{t+1}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}\ket{y}\otimes W_{t+1}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}.

Next, for every
x\in V
, we calculate (again invoking
Eq.

5.14
)

\displaystyle Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t}F)\left(\ket{x}\otimes\Theta_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}\left(\ket{x}\otimes W_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}\right)

(5.20)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}\ket{y}\otimes W_{t+1}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}W_{t}^{+}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}W_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}

(5.21)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}\ket{y}\otimes W_{t+1}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}},

(5.22)

where the last equation holds because
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
and
W_{t}^{+}
commute and
W_{t}^{+}W_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}=\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
.

Inserting an additional
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
on the right, we conclude that

Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t}F)(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\Theta_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}})=(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t+1}F)Q_{\rho}(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes\Theta_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}),

and thus

Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t}F)=(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t+1}F)Q_{\rho}

when restricted to inputs in
V\otimes\im(\Theta_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}})=V\otimes F^{-1}(\mathsf{Tab}_{t})
, which is the first claim of
Theorem

5.3
.

Quantum query algorithms

To obtain the second claim of
Theorem

5.3
, we note that all recording states obtainable by making
t
queries to
Q_{\rho}
lie in
F^{-1}(\mathsf{Tab}_{t})
.

Lemma 5.7

(Reachable states are in
\im(\Theta_{t})
)
.

Fix any adaptive quantum algorithm whose query register is
V
and queries
Q_{\rho}
. Then, its recording register always remains in
\im(\Theta_{t})=F^{-1}(\mathsf{Tab}_{t})
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
Eq.

5.19
and an induction on
t
.
∎

Finally, we observe that in this context, the one-query identity implies the
t
-query statement by induction. In particular, suppose that immediately before the
(t+1)
-st query, the path-recorded
simulation state is obtained from the uncompressed representation oracle by
applying

\mathbf{I}\otimes U_{t}F

to the recording register. The adversary’s next local unitary commutes with the recording-register
isometry. The next oracle call is handled by the identity

Q_{\rho,t+1}^{\mathrm{Path}}(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t}F)=(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t+1}F)Q_{\rho}.

Therefore the invariant is preserved with
t+1
in place of
t
.

Starting from the trivial depth-
0
recording register, induction over the

t
queries gives

A^{Q_{\rho}}(\ket{\psi})\simeq A^{Q_{\rho}^{\mathrm{Path}}}(\ket{\psi})

via the final isometry
U_{t}F
. This proves the second claim of
Theorem

5.3
.

5.6
Transpose queries

The same argument also gives a path-recording update for transpose queries.
Here transpose is taken with respect to the fixed basis
\mathcal{B}(V)
.

Theorem 5.8

(Transpose path-recording update)
.

Let
Q_{\rho^{\top}}
denote the purified oracle which applies
\rho(g)^{\top}

to the adversary’s query register:

Q_{\rho^{\top}}\left(|x\rangle\otimes\int_{G}f(g)|g\rangle\,dg\right)=\int_{G}f(g)\,\rho(g)^{\top}|x\rangle\otimes|g\rangle\,dg.

Then, for every
x\in\mathcal{B}(V)
,

Q_{\rho^{\top}}\cdot(|x\rangle\otimes\Theta_{t})=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{B}(V)}|y\rangle\otimes\Theta_{t+1}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{y,x}.

Consequently, if we define

Q^{\mathrm{Path},\top}_{\rho,t+1}:=\sum_{x,y\in\mathcal{B}(V)}\outerproduct{y}{x}\otimes W_{t+1}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{y,x}W_{t}^{+}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}},

then

Q^{\mathrm{Path},\top}_{\rho,t+1}\cdot(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t}F)=(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t+1}F)\cdot Q_{\rho^{\top}}

on inputs restricted to
V\otimes F^{-1}(\mathsf{Tab}_{t})
.

Proof.

First we prove the matrix-element identity. By
definition,

\Theta_{t}|\vec{x},\vec{y}\rangle=\int_{G}\langle\vec{y}|\rho(g)^{\otimes t}|\vec{x}\rangle|g\rangle\,dg.

Therefore

\displaystyle Q_{\rho^{\top}}\left(|x\rangle\otimes\Theta_{t}|\vec{x},\vec{y}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{B}(V)}|y\rangle\otimes\int_{G}\langle y|\rho(g)^{\top}|x\rangle\langle\vec{y}|\rho(g)^{\otimes t}|\vec{x}\rangle|g\rangle\,dg

(5.23)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{B}(V)}|y\rangle\otimes\int_{G}\langle x|\rho(g)|y\rangle\langle\vec{y}|\rho(g)^{\otimes t}|\vec{x}\rangle|g\rangle\,dg

(5.24)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{B}(V)}|y\rangle\otimes\Theta_{t+1}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{y,x}|\vec{x},\vec{y}\rangle.

(5.25)

Thus a transpose query appends the reversed pair
(y,x)
. We now prove the path-recording identity. By
Eq.

5.14
,

U_{t}F\Theta_{t}=W_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}},\qquad U_{t+1}F\Theta_{t+1}=W_{t+1}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}.

Applying
\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t+1}F
to the transpose matrix-element identity gives

(\mathbf{I}_{V}\otimes U_{t+1}F)Q_{\rho^{\top}}(|x\rangle\otimes\Theta_{t})=\sum_{y}|y\rangle\otimes W_{t+1}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{y,x}.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle Q^{\mathrm{Path},\top}_{\rho,t+1}(|x\rangle\otimes W_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}|y\rangle\otimes W_{t+1}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{y,x}W_{t}^{+}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}W_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}

(5.26)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y}|y\rangle\otimes W_{t+1}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t+1}}\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{y,x}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}},

(5.27)

using
W_{t}^{+}W_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}=\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
. Therefore the two expressions agree
when restricted to inputs in

V\otimes\operatorname{Im}(\Theta_{t}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}})=V\otimes F^{-1}(\mathsf{Tab}_{t}),

which proves the theorem.
∎

Thus arbitrary adaptive algorithms making forward and transpose queries are
simulated by using
\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{x,y}
for a forward query and

\operatorname{App}^{(t)}_{y,x}
for a transpose query.

5.7
Schur Orthogonality for the Commutant Algebras

Our next step is to
interpret
the commutant EPR projector
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}
. To do this, we first show that whenever the commutant algebra is semisimple, it satisfies the Schur orthogonality relations for the matrix elements of its simple modules.

Theorem 5.9

(Schur Orthogonality)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a semisimple algebra, and let
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{A})
be a basis for
\mathcal{A}
.
For
a\in\mathcal{A}
, let
\tr(a)
be the trace of the regular representation of
\mathcal{A}
.
For each
a\in\mathcal{A}
, let
a^{*}
be its dual element under this trace.
Let
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda(\mathcal{A})
be the labels of two simple modules,
V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\lambda}
and
V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu}
, of
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
\ket{a},\ket{b}\in\mathcal{B}(V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\lambda})
and
\ket{c},\ket{d}\in\mathcal{B}(V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu})
.
Then
43
43

43

Note that compared with the Schur orthogonality theorem for irrep matrix elements of
groups
, the second matrix element is transposed rather than complex conjugated. As we mention below in
Remark

5.10
, this is because for groups,
\sigma^{*}
is proportional to its inverse, which of course conjugate-transposes the irrep.

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{A})}\bra{a}\rho_{\lambda}(\sigma)\ket{b}\;\bra{c}\rho_{\mu}(\sigma^{*})\ket{d}=\frac{1}{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\lambda})}\delta_{\lambda\mu}\delta_{ad}\delta_{bc}

(5.28)

Proof.

Consider the matrix

T_{bc}\coloneqq\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{A})}\rho_{\lambda}(\sigma)\cdot\outerproduct{b}{c}\cdot\rho_{\mu}(\sigma^{*}).

Our goal is to show that

T_{bc}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\lambda})}\mathbf{I}_{V^{\lambda}_{\mathcal{A}}}&\text{if $\lambda=\mu$ and $b=c$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

If we can show this, then

\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{A})}\bra{a}\rho_{\lambda}(\sigma)\ket{b}\;\bra{c}\rho_{\mu}(\sigma^{*})\ket{d}=\bra{a}T_{bc}\ket{d}

is equal to

\frac{1}{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\lambda})}\bra{a}\mathbf{I}_{M_{\lambda}}\ket{d}=\frac{1}{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\lambda})}\innerproduct{a}{d}=\frac{1}{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\lambda})}\delta_{ad}

if
\lambda=\mu
and
b=c
and is equal to
0
otherwise.
This gives the desired equality.

To prove this, we observe that by
[
HR05
, Proposition 5.2(b)]
, applied with

M=V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu}
,
N=V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\lambda}
, and

\phi=\outerproduct{b}{c}
, the map
T_{b,c}
is a
\mathcal{A}
-module homomorphism, meaning that for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

T_{bc}\rho_{\mu}(a)=\rho_{\lambda}(a)T_{bc}.

We now consider two cases.

Case 1:
\lambda\neq\mu

If
\lambda\neq\mu
, Schur’s lemma (
[
HR05
, Proposition 5.3(2)]
) states that
T_{bc}=0
.

Case 2:
\lambda=\mu

In this case, Schur’s lemma (
[
HR05
, Proposition 5.3(1)]
) states that
T_{bc}=\alpha\cdot\mathbf{I}_{V^{\lambda}_{\mathcal{A}}}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
.
Taking the (linear algebraic) trace of both sides, we have that

\tr(T_{bc})=\alpha\cdot\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\lambda}),

from which we see that
\alpha=\tr(T_{bc})/\dim(V^{\lambda}_{\mathcal{A}})
.
It therefore suffices to compute the trace of
T_{bc}
.

To compute this, we write

\displaystyle\tr(T_{bc})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{A})}\tr(\rho_\lambda(\sigma) \cdot\ketbra{b}{c} \cdot\rho_\lambda(\sigma^*))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{A})}\tr( \ketbra{b}{c} \cdot\rho_\lambda(\sigma^*) \cdot\rho_\lambda(\sigma))

\displaystyle=\tr( \ketbra{b}{c} \cdot\rho_\lambda\Big(\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal B(\mathcal A)} \sigma^*\sigma\Big))

\displaystyle=\tr(\ketbra{b}{c})

\displaystyle=\delta_{bc},

where we have invoked the identity
\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{A})}\sigma^{*}\sigma=1

[
HR05
, Proof of Theorem 5.8]
.
∎
An alternative proof of
Theorem

5.9
also appears in
[
FND26
, Corollary 2.12]
.

Remark 5.10
.

Note that this generalizes the Schur orthogonality for groups, which states that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{G}}\sum_{g\in G}\bra{a}\rho_{\lambda}(g)\ket{b}\;\overline{\bra{d}\rho_{\mu}(g)\ket{c}}=\sum_{g\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C}[G])}\bra{a}\rho_{\lambda}(g)\ket{b}\;\bra{c}\rho_{\mu}\left(\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{G}}g^{-1}\right)\ket{d}=\frac{1}{\dim(V_{\mathcal{A}}^{\lambda})}\delta_{\lambda\mu}\delta_{ad}\delta_{bc}

(5.29)

since note that whenever
\mathcal{A}
is a group algebra
\mathbb{C}[G]
, then we have that,
g^{*}=\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{G}}g^{-1}\quad\forall g\in G
(since
\frac{1}{\absolutevalue{G}}\tr(h^{-1} g)=\delta_{hg}
).

5.8
Interpretation of the Commutant EPR projector

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\rho:G\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(V)
be a representation, and consider the tensor product representation
\rho^{\otimes t}
on
V^{\otimes t}
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{t}
